# 1\. 「君を照らすムーンライトロード」 — Mei as supporter of Kotono’s return

**Importance for Mei: important, but primarily a Tsuki/Kotono event.**

This event is not Mei-centered, but it matters because it places Mei inside Moon Tempest’s mature ethic of trust.

When Sakura learns that Kotono left Moon Tempest, she wants to stop her. The remaining Moon Tempest members explain why they do not: they are lonely, hurt, and do not want Kotono gone, but they also believe that a true friend must trust Kotono’s decision and send her forward. Mei’s contribution is simple and morally direct: if Kotono decided after that much suffering and resolve, forcing her to stay would be wrong; a real friend believes in her, supports her, and sends her off. CORE\_008\_\_event\_2024\_006\_st-eve…

This is very Mei. She does not give the most elaborate argument. Nagisa supplies the relational faith; Saki supplies the ethical seriousness; Suzu supplies the proud defense of Kotono’s resolve. Mei supplies the blunt emotional truth: **friendship is not possession**.

Her later line is also important: Moon Tempest will not merely “protect” Kotono’s place; they will become a group Kotono will want to return to. CORE\_008\_\_event\_2024\_006\_st-eve… That is Mei’s support ethic in a more active form. Support does not mean waiting passively. It means becoming stronger so the person who left can return to something worthy.

Later, when Kotono is rattled by online criticism and audience hostility, Mei’s contribution is again simple and embodied: “let’s go with idol-like smiles,” “everyone smile.” CORE\_008\_\_event\_2024\_006\_st-eve… On the page, that sounds almost childish. In context, it is Mei doing what she always does: returning the group to the body, the face, the immediate act of performance. Where Kotono internalizes pressure, Mei gives a performable instruction: smile.

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei’s support is not abstract comfort. It is action: send Kotono forward, strengthen the group, smile on stage, and make the moment survivable.

---

# 2\. 「月夜に輝く恋の魔法」 — Mei creates Moon Tempest’s summer

**Importance for Mei: important to core.**

This is one of the strongest Mei creative-process events.

The premise begins with Moon Tempest invited to a major beach festival. Mei is the one who senses the mismatch between the opportunity and their existing repertoire. She argues that a beach festival needs something surprising, something summery, something special — and then proposes a new summer song for Moon Tempest. IMPORTANT\_002\_\_event\_2021\_002\_s…

At first, her argument looks impulsive. Everyone else worries about time, quality, and concept. But Mei is not wrong. Her intuition is that **the occasion itself demands a new emotional form**. A standard Moon Tempest set would be competent, but not alive to the place. Mei understands performance as situational: beach, summer, festival, night, audience expectation. She wants the stage to respond to where it is.

The story then lets Mei fail productively. She brings the group to the sea, they spend the day eating shaved ice and playing, and by evening they still cannot define “Moon Tempest’s summer.” But that “wasted” day becomes the necessary experience. Mei’s method is not bad because it is inefficient; it is experiential. The unit has to feel summer before it can sing summer.

The breakthrough comes during the fireworks jinx conversation: confession under fireworks, moonlight, lovers, the courage to act before the moment disappears. Mei synthesizes the scattered motifs into a song premise: a girl who has loved someone for a long time but could not say it, and who decides to confess before the fireworks end. IMPORTANT\_002\_\_event\_2021\_002\_s…

This is crucial because it shows Mei’s creative intelligence in its natural form. She does not build concepts through literary analysis like Nagisa or disciplined abstraction like Saki. She builds concepts by collecting sensations and emotional sparks until they suddenly ignite.

The final pre-live speech is one of Mei’s best statements of Moon Tempest’s identity. She says they are not “The Summer!” like Sunny Peace, but their new song is Moon Tempest’s own summer. Night is necessary for fireworks and the moon to look beautiful; they may not be dazzling like the sun, but there is a beauty that exists precisely because it is not sunlight. IMPORTANT\_002\_\_event\_2021\_002\_s…

That is an unusually mature Mei formulation. She turns her own “summer sun” temperament into a defense of Moon Tempest’s lunar identity.

The ending, where Mei realizes she is “probably in love” with Moon Tempest, is not just a gag. She says she wants to stay with everyone and stand on stage with them; the feeling of wanting to be with the people she loves resembles romantic feeling to her. IMPORTANT\_002\_\_event\_2021\_002\_s… This is one of the earliest clean articulations of Mei’s broad love vocabulary: she experiences group devotion, friendship, and romantic longing as overlapping forms of “好き.”

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei is a genuine concept-generator. She can discover a unit’s emotional direction through play, sensation, and sudden association. She also explicitly names her attachment to Moon Tempest as love — not romantic in the strict sense, but intense, embodied, and stage-directed.

---

# 3\. 「迷走ピリオド 涼やかな青春」 — Mei and Suzu learn to fight honestly

**Importance for Mei: core.**

This is probably the most important event here for Mei’s relationship architecture.

At the start, Mei and Suzu look like an effortless comic duo. Mei tickles Suzu backstage to release her tension, shares donuts with her, and invites her into a two-person unit because she thinks it would be fun. Their closeness is obvious: they are described as having twin-like chemistry, and Suzu even calls herself the one with the densest relationship with Mei. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

But the event’s real subject is not harmony. It is the danger of harmony becoming unspoken assumption.

Mei is far more competent here than Suzu expects. She immediately thinks about meetings, costumes, choreography, lyrics, interviews, scheduling, and production flow, explaining that she learned from working with Rei and now wants to take initiative herself. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s… This is an important professional growth marker: Mei is not just a raw ball of instinct. She has learned from prior collaborations and wants to carry that forward.

The conflict begins because Mei and Suzu are trying to support each other in incompatible ways. Suzu wants to bring out Mei’s maximum potential, so her ideas are built around Mei’s high dance ability and “cool” force. Mei wants to create something that combines both of them, so her ideas are more “cool-cute,” accessible, and mutual. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

This is one of the most revealing parts of Mei’s character. She is not choosing an easier concept because she lacks ambition. She is choosing it because she understands the unit as **two people**, not Mei plus support. Her concept is relationally intelligent: if the unit is Mei/Suzu, the stage should contain Mei and Suzu’s strengths together.

Suzu misreads this as Mei holding back. That hurts Mei because Mei has been thinking seriously too. When Suzu says Mei’s approach denies her effort, Mei quietly answers that she has also been trying hard and thinking through many things. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

Then Mana’s remembered advice becomes the hinge of the story. Mana tells Mei that even kind people can accumulate unsaid feelings, and that she herself could speak honestly to the manager because he got seriously angry at her when she overworked herself. Mana hopes Mei someday finds someone she can truly collide with when her heart becomes full. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

That line is vital. Mei’s kindness is not shallow cheer; it can become self-erasure if she never pushes back. Mana gives Mei permission to fight.

The confrontation with Suzu is therefore a maturation event. Mei comes back early because she wants to see Suzu and says directly that she came to fight. She tells Suzu she is angry, that Suzu stopped listening, that Suzu’s mood made her not know what to do, and that Suzu is an idiot. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s… The fight is comic, but emotionally serious. Mei finally stops converting discomfort into cheer.

The repair is beautiful because Mei does not end with accusation. After listing everything, she says that all of it included, she loves Suzu. She is happy to hear Suzu’s feelings, apologizes, admits she did not want them to stop talking, and confesses that she worries she says strange things and might be a bother. Suzu admits she too was lonely when they went home separately. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

The final concept resolution confirms Mei’s original intuition: Suzu had been thinking only about Mei; Mei had been trying to combine both their strengths. Together they produce a song that includes both jealousy and “twins,” both personal friction and shared affection, and finally the Mana-linked “shiny shiny.” IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

**What it adds to Mei:**
This event proves Mei’s emotional maturity requires conflict, not just cheer. Her deepest friendship is not the one where no one fights; it is the one where she can finally say, “I’m angry,” and still end with, “I love you.”

---

# 4\. 「君を照らすムーンライトロード」 and Mei’s Moon Tempest role after return

**Importance for Mei: secondary but structurally important.**

This overlaps with the first section, but it is worth isolating what the event does for Mei within Moon Tempest’s mature public identity.

After Kotono’s return, the event defines Moon Tempest’s larger answer: their stage can illuminate someone else’s road. The former idol opponent says that losing to Moon Tempest gave her the courage to move forward and retire without regret; Kotono realizes that Moon Tempest illuminated a person who was lost. CORE\_008\_\_event\_2024\_006\_st-eve…

For Mei, this matters because her instinctive “make people smile” function is now incorporated into the unit’s larger moonlight philosophy. Mei alone often brightens the moment; Moon Tempest as a whole now gives people courage to continue, stop, change direction, or move forward.

The event also clarifies that Mei’s warmth is not opposed to Moon Tempest’s serious identity. In fact, her simple “smile” language is one of the ways Moon Tempest stays human under pressure. Kotono can articulate the moonlight philosophy; Nagisa can emotionalize it; Saki can discipline it; Suzu can dramatize it; Mei keeps it performable.

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei’s smile ethic becomes part of Moon Tempest’s mature philosophy. She is not an exception to the unit’s seriousness; she is one of the mechanisms by which its seriousness remains alive and warm.

# 5\. 「ぶつかり愛のCenter Battle！」 — Mei wants the center

**Importance for Mei: core.**

This event is essential for Mei because it breaks the lazy reading that Mei is content to be the cheerful support member.

When the new Valentine song becomes an opportunity to choose a center from all five members, Mei immediately volunteers. This could initially sound like her usual “me too!” energy, but the story quickly shows that she is deeply serious. She organizes morning and night practice, cuts into donut-shop time, and says she is absolutely not going to lose. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve…

Her motivation is not vanity. During practice, her inner monologue says she wants to shine for the fans, the manager, and Moon Tempest. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve… That matters because Mei’s ambition is relational. She wants the center because the center is where her desire to make others happy becomes most directly visible.

When Nagisa wins, Mei tries to do the correct celebratory thing — drinks, donuts, a party mood. Suzu sees through it and follows her. Then Mei collapses into tears. She says she is happy for Nagisa, but her heart tightens; she is frustrated; she wanted to be center. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve…

This is one of Mei’s most important emotional scenes because it proves she can hold contradictory feelings without one canceling the other. She genuinely loves Nagisa and genuinely wanted to beat her. That is a mature idol emotion.

Even more important is the reason she wanted it. Mei says she did not merely want to be a member; she wanted to be the center, like Suzu. She wanted to support everyone the way Suzu supported the four-member Moon Tempest when Kotono was gone. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve… This reframes Mei’s ambition as inheritance: not “I want attention,” but “I want to become the person who can carry others.”

Then she articulates her ideal self: cool like Suzu, and able to make everyone happy like Mana. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve… This line is probably one of the best single-sentence keys to Mei’s aspiration. Suzu gives her the model of internal unit support; Mana gives her the model of idol happiness.

The aftermath also matters. Mei does not sulk. She contributes MC and costume ideas for Nagisa’s center stage almost immediately. CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve… That is Mei’s growth: she can lose, cry, admit frustration, and then turn back toward the group with useful energy.

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei is ambitious. She wants to be center. She wants to be central not for ego alone, but because she wants to become a support pillar — the kind of idol who can make others happy and carry the unit when needed.

---

# 6\. 「未来へ続く夏祭り」 — Mei and the joy of transient time

**Importance for Mei: important.**

This event is not as Mei-centered as the Suzu and center stories, but it deepens a subtler part of her: her relationship to fleeting happiness.

The surface setup is classic Moon Tempest comedy: everyone has procrastinated on summer homework except Saki, and Mei’s reason is blunt — homework is boring. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s… But once the homework is finished, the group gets to play by the sea, meet Miku, and turn summer leisure into a creative collaboration.

Mei’s first major contribution is social. After Moon Tempest meets Miku, Mei immediately proposes a friendship gathering because she wants to get closer to her. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s… That is Mei’s default diplomacy: closeness is created by doing something together, not by formal reverence.

The event’s emotional center is Miku’s question: why do people feel lonely when summer ends if summer will come again? Mei initially cannot explain it. She simply feels it. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s… The others then articulate what Mei embodies instinctively: this summer is only this summer; being with Miku in this moment is precious; loneliness is not the same as sadness. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s…

This matters for Mei because it refines her “do it again” worldview. Mei often responds to happiness by wanting repetition: another donut, another phone call, another beach day, another performance. This story teaches a more delicate version: repetition is good, but the present moment is precious because it cannot be repeated exactly.

The resolution is also very Moon Tempest: if the feeling is hard to explain, put it into a song. Mei says that if they put the feeling into music, they can absolutely communicate it. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s… On stage, she voices the simple form of the feeling: she understands not wanting summer to end, wanting to play more, and feeling lonely. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s…

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei’s joy gains bittersweetness. She is not only the girl who wants fun to continue forever; she is learning that the sadness of ending can itself become part of the joy, and that song can carry feelings too subtle for her usual vocabulary.

---

# 7\. 「IDOLY MATCH～地下闘技場への挑戦～」 — Mei discovers idolhood outside legitimacy

**Importance for Mei: important to core.**

This event looks ridiculous, but it is thematically useful.

Mei first enters the story through concern and action. Saki worries that Aoi is sneaking out at night; Mei immediately says to leave it to her and promises to find out what Aoi is doing. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s… This is standard Mei: if someone is worried, she moves.

When she discovers the underground idol arena, her first response is delight: she does not fully understand it, but the phrase sounds cool. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s… Aoi explains that the arena is for “wild stray idols” outside the VENUS Program — people who still want to shine as idols despite not belonging to official structures. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

This is where Mei’s openness matters. Rei initially reacts with suspicion and status-conscious horror; Mei is receptive. The arena is hot, chaotic, weird, and unofficial — but Mei can still feel idol energy there. She later says the performances make her body restless and that she wants to sing there too. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

The preparation material is comic but meaningful. Mei, Rei, and Aoi have to create names, music, costumes, and a disguised performance outside their normal support systems. Mei’s naming ideas are absurd, but the whole sequence places her in a DIY idol context. She is not merely consuming spectacle; she wants to participate.

The climax against BEIJU, the elderly idol group, provides the key lesson. Mei cheers for the opponent because watching them makes her chest heat up and makes her want to support them. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s… Rei and Aoi realize that although their own singing and dancing may be technically superior, BEIJU’s stage makes the audience want to cheer more strongly. Aoi concludes that they are real idols: no matter their age, they challenge themselves, work hard, grow with friends, and entertain the audience with everything they have. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

This is a major idol-philosophy correction for all three, but especially for Mei. It confirms that her own instinct — “I want to cheer for them” — is not naïve. It detects something real.

The final reflection is equally important. The trio accepts that in VENUS scoring they probably would have won, but the underground arena’s criterion is not technical scoring by AI. It is whether the audience was entertained and who felt like the more wonderful idol. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s… Mei then clarifies that her own place is still the VENUS stage, where her beloved friends and fans are waiting. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

**What it adds to Mei:**
Mei’s idol sense is legitimacy-agnostic but home-aware. She can recognize idol light anywhere — even in an absurd underground battle ring — but she also knows where her own light belongs: with Moon Tempest, Hoshimi, and the fans waiting for her.

# Cross-event synthesis: Mei after these stories

## 1\. Mei’s seriousness is now undeniable

These events move Mei beyond “cheerful, funny, energetic.” She is still those things, but now she is also someone who:

-   proposes major creative direction;

-   cuts donut time for center practice;

-   cries from genuine frustration;

-   wants to become the unit’s center;

-   fights Suzu because she cannot bear emotional distance;

-   learns from Mana’s advice about honest conflict;

-   recognizes idol value outside official rankings;

-   and turns fleeting seasonal emotion into song.


Her seriousness does not look like Kotono’s burden, Saki’s discipline, or Suzu’s pride. It looks like **full-body commitment**.

## 2\. Suzu is Mei’s most important peer mirror

The Mei/Suzu relationship becomes central here. Suzu is not merely Mei’s comic partner. She is:

-   Mei’s “twin-like” partner;

-   her strongest chaos resonance;

-   someone who indulges and corrects her;

-   someone Mei admires as a center;

-   someone Mei wants to support;

-   someone Mei can finally fight honestly;

-   and someone whose jealousy forces Mei to articulate her own care.


Suzu gives Mei a path from undifferentiated friendliness to chosen intimacy. Mei can be friendly with everyone, but with Suzu she learns what it means to collide.

## 3\. Mana is Mei’s ideal of happy idolhood

These events clarify Mei’s Mana-model.

Mei does not relate to Mana as a frozen saint. She remembers Mana as someone she spent time with, shopped with, laughed with, and learned from. In the Suzu event, Mana’s remembered advice gives Mei permission to express pent-up feelings. In the center event, Mei explicitly wants to become someone who can make everyone happy like Mana. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s… CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve…

So for Mei, Mana is not “the unreachable miracle” as much as “the person who made idol happiness feel real.”

## 4\. Mei’s creative method is experiential

Mei rarely begins with a polished concept. She begins with “this feels fun,” “this place needs something,” “this stage should surprise people,” “let’s go to the beach,” or “I want to sing there too.”

But that does not mean she is shallow. Her creativity works through:

1.  sensory immersion;

2.  emotional association;

3.  sudden conceptual synthesis;

4.  immediate action;

5.  group refinement.


That is how she creates the summer confession song, the Mei/Suzu “cool-cute” unit, and her contribution to the Miku summer-end collaboration.

## 5\. Mei’s idol philosophy is audience-first, but not score-first

The underground arena event is especially useful because it separates technical excellence from idol impact. Mei instinctively wants to cheer BEIJU because their stage makes her chest heat up. The story validates that feeling: being an idol is not only about measurable performance quality; it is about making the audience want to believe, support, and smile. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

This loops back to Mei’s own idol type. Mei may not always be the most technically polished or conceptually refined, but she has the crucial idol instinct: she knows when a performance reaches people.

## 6\. Mei’s “love” vocabulary becomes more credible, not less

The summer confession event could be dismissed as Mei misunderstanding romance, but when placed beside the center battle and Suzu event, it becomes more meaningful.

Mei’s “love” may be category-unstable, but it is not fake. She loves Moon Tempest, Suzu, Mana’s ideal, fans, the manager, stage time, donuts, cats, and shared memories with real force. The issue is not whether Mei feels deeply. The issue is that her feelings are broader than the conventional labels she has available.

That makes her emotionally distinctive:

**Mei often knows the force of a feeling before she knows its name.**

---

# Importance tiers for this Mei batch

## Core

**「迷走ピリオド 涼やかな青春」**
Core Mei/Suzu event. It proves Mei can be hurt, angry, jealous, and serious; it gives her a model from Mana for honest conflict; and it transforms Mei/Suzu from easy chaos duo into a relationship where both can collide sincerely. IMPORTANT\_015\_\_event\_2023\_010\_s…

**「ぶつかり愛のCenter Battle！」**
Core Mei ambition event. It shows Mei wanting center, working with unusual discipline, crying from frustration, admiring Suzu’s center role, and defining her ideal as “cool like Suzu” and “making everyone happy like Mana.” CORE\_009\_\_event\_2026\_002\_st-eve…

## Important to core

**「月夜に輝く恋の魔法」**
Important Mei creative-process event. Mei creates the premise for Moon Tempest’s summer song and explicitly names her love for Moon Tempest as something close to romantic feeling. IMPORTANT\_002\_\_event\_2021\_002\_s… +1

**「IDOLY MATCH～地下闘技場への挑戦～」**
Important idol-philosophy event. Mei recognizes idol energy outside official structures and learns, through BEIJU, that making people want to cheer can matter more than technical superiority. IMPORTANT\_026\_\_event\_2026\_004\_s…

## Important

**「君を照らすムーンライトロード」**
Important for Mei as part of Moon Tempest’s mature support ethic. She helps define friendship as trusting Kotono’s decision and later functions as a simple smile/morale anchor under pressure. CORE\_008\_\_event\_2024\_006\_st-eve… +1

**「未来へ続く夏祭り」**
Important for Mei’s relation to fleeting joy. It teaches that summer’s sadness is not merely negative; it is part of the preciousness of the moment, and song can communicate feelings too subtle for ordinary explanation. IMPORTANT\_024\_\_event\_2025\_008\_s… +1

---

# Updated Mei thesis after these events

**Hayasaka Mei is Moon Tempest’s kinetic sun, but these events show that her brightness is not shallow cheer. It is a serious, full-force way of being an idol. Mei experiences emotion as motion: proposing a new song, dragging the group to the sea, fighting Suzu because silence hurts, cutting donut time to practice center, cheering for an opponent because their stage reaches her, and trying to turn the end of summer into music.**

**Her deepest growth is not becoming calm. It is learning that joy becomes stronger when it includes rivalry, disappointment, conflict, grief, limitation, and endings. She wants to make people happy like Mana, support Moon Tempest like Suzu once did, and stand in the center not because she wants attention alone, but because she wants to carry everyone’s happiness from the brightest possible place.**

Does this one meaningfully add to Mei?

**Assistant — Turn 52**

Yes — **meaningfully, but not as a top-tier Mei event in the same way as the Mei/Suzu conflict event or Center Battle**.

I would classify it as **Important**, not **Core**, for Mei. It is probably **Core for Rei**, and **Important-to-core for Mei/Rei**.

# What it adds to Mei

The big addition is that this event gives an early, concrete form of Mei’s **relational intervention instinct**.

In later material, we already saw Mei notice that Rei’s dance feels tense and try to get Rei’s father to watch her. This event is the earlier, fuller version of that pattern: Mei hears about Rei’s family pain, notices Rei’s hidden desire to be seen by her parents, and immediately decides, “we have to do something.” Rei says her parents will never come, but Mei sees that Rei still wants recognition. After Rei leaves, Mei tells Suzu that Rei probably wants her parents to come, and that Rei looked pained even if she did not show it openly. IMPORTANT\_005\_\_event\_2021\_005\_s…

That is very useful for Mei because it proves her emotional intelligence is not purely “cheerful intuition.” She can read suppressed hurt in someone as guarded as Rei.

The second meaningful addition is the **family contrast**. Mei says she assumed everyone’s family supported them because her own family supports her. That helps explain Mei’s emotional abundance: unlike Rei, whose idolhood is tied to paternal disapproval, Mei comes from a secure support base. Her default expectation is that family should cheer for your stage. When she realizes that is not true for Rei, she is genuinely shocked. IMPORTANT\_005\_\_event\_2021\_005\_s…

The third addition is that Mei’s care is shown as **boundary-crossing but sincere**. She and Suzu literally go to Rei’s family home to invite her parents. Mei even knows the address because she had peeked into Rei’s room, which is very Mei: ethically messy, socially bizarre, but emotionally motivated. She sees a problem and moves before fully considering whether the method is appropriate. IMPORTANT\_005\_\_event\_2021\_005\_s…

The fourth addition is interesting because Mei is not only chaos here. When Suzu explodes at Rei’s father, Mei is the one who tries to stop her and de-escalate. That is a good corrective to reading Mei as always the least restrained person in the room. In this specific emotional configuration, Suzu becomes the volcanic one and Mei becomes the practical brake. IMPORTANT\_005\_\_event\_2021\_005\_s…

The fifth addition is that Mei’s concern can impair her performance. During the live, when Rei believes her father has not come, Mei becomes so worried about Rei that she loses focus and even forgets the new song’s title. This is a small but important flaw: Mei’s empathy is powerful, but not always regulated. Her care can pull her out of professional mode. IMPORTANT\_005\_\_event\_2021\_005\_s…

# What it does *not* add

It does **not** radically redefine Mei’s core. It mostly reinforces things already established elsewhere:

-   Mei acts before analyzing.

-   Mei wants people to be happy together.

-   Mei notices when someone is hiding pain.

-   Mei’s family support is unusually uncomplicated.

-   Mei believes idol performance can reach people.

-   Mei’s care is clumsy, direct, and embodied.


Also, the event’s decisive ideological lines belong more to **Rei and Suzu** than to Mei. Rei articulates that idols can save people and that her happiness must be self-chosen. Suzu gives the strongest defense of Rei’s pride and escorts the father into the venue. Mei is the catalyst and emotional bridge, not the final spokesperson.

# How I would classify it

**For Mei: Important.**
It is especially important as the origin/early form of the Rei-Mei “MACARON DONUTS” relationship and as a precursor to Mei’s later Rei-focused card.

**For Rei: Core.**
This is a major family/father/idol legitimacy event.

**For Mei/Rei: Important-to-core.**
It shows Mei as the person who notices Rei’s suppressed wish and pushes the situation into motion.

# Best one-line update

**This event adds Mei as a bridge-maker: a girl whose secure family love makes her unable to ignore Rei’s lack of recognition, and whose instinctive care pushes Rei toward the confrontation she secretly needs.**

**Assistant — Turn 54**

I expanded the archive and inspected **22 Mei card illustrations**. I also made a review sheet here: Mei card contact sheet.

## Core visual impression

Mei’s visual identity is **kinetic warmth**. She is designed less like a distant idol to be admired from below and more like a bright force that rushes directly toward the viewer. Nearly every image gives the impression of motion, appetite, contact, or immediate delight: donuts flying through the frame, cats climbing on her, water splashing around her, fireworks sparking in her hands, presents spilling from a sleigh, stage ribbons curving around her body, or neon signs exploding behind her.

Her visual thesis is:

**Mei looks like joy in motion — a girl whose feelings become physical action before they become words.**

That makes her very different from Kotono and Nagisa. Kotono is composed moonlight. Nagisa is affectionate memory. Mei is the moment when the scene suddenly starts moving.

---

# Hair / eyes / silhouette

Mei’s hair is a warm caramel/cinnamon brown, often lit with honey-gold highlights. It is one of the most important parts of her design because it rarely sits still. Her bangs are soft and slightly messy, with loose side strands and a high side-tail or ponytail arrangement that often flares outward. In action cards, her hair behaves almost like a motion trail: it arcs, bounces, lifts, and spreads with her movement.

This gives her a more energetic silhouette than Nagisa’s rounded bob or Kotono’s long, elegant vertical line. Mei’s hair makes her feel **springy**, as if she is always mid-leap or about to run somewhere.

Her eyes are large, reddish amber/pink-brown, and very open. They are usually bright, glossy, and emotionally immediate. Mei’s gaze tends to be direct: she looks at the viewer like she is inviting them into something right now. Even when she is surprised or softer, her eyes rarely feel guarded. They communicate trust before analysis.

Her silhouette is athletic, compact, and dynamic. She is often posed diagonally, reaching forward, falling backward, kicking through water, leaning into the frame, or twisting mid-performance. The art almost never lets her become static. Even sitting or lying down, she still feels like she has just moved or is about to move again.

---

# Costume and color language

Mei’s palette is the warmest and most candy-colored of the Moon Tempest girls. She strongly favors:

**red, orange, pink, white, cream, yellow, warm brown, turquoise, candy blue, and bright festival colors.**

Her recurring visual motifs are equally clear:

**donuts, cats, sweets, stars, ribbons, water, fireworks, presents, sporty clothing, summer props, bright stage lighting, and playful accessories.**

The birthday donut card is almost the purest Mei image: red-white-blue diner colors, roller skates, trays of donuts, soda-fountain motifs, a wink, and a body thrown diagonally into the viewer’s space. It is not merely “Mei likes donuts.” The image turns donuts into a public idol identity: food, service, motion, hospitality, fan contact, and joyful excess all at once.

Her summer cards use water, sunlight, fireworks, crabs, beach imagery, and splashing motion. These reinforce the “internal sun” reading of Mei within Moon Tempest. Moon Tempest is thematically lunar, but Mei repeatedly brings in sunlight, summer glare, water-sparkle, and festival heat. She is not outside the group identity; she animates it from within.

Her darker or cooler costume concepts are especially revealing. In the rock/underground/neon cards, Mei can wear black, leather-like accents, caution tape, cyber-neon, and punk styling, but the design still keeps her readable as Mei: open eyes, forward motion, bright contrast colors, and a sense of playful danger rather than true menace. Even “cool Mei” is still kinetic and approachable.

Her wedding card is the largest tonal gap. The light is soft, white, floral, and ceremonial; her expression becomes shy, quiet, and almost reverent. But even there, the image does not erase Mei. It shows that beneath her chaos, she can become emotionally serious when the scene asks for future, family, gratitude, and “ずっと一緒” intimacy.

---

# Body language / facial expression tendencies

Mei’s body language is overwhelmingly open.

She reaches toward the viewer. She winks. She spreads her arms. She kicks through water. She leans forward over tables. She throws herself into scenes. She holds sparklers close to the camera. She looks like someone who experiences the world by touching it.

Her most common expressions are:

**wide open-mouth smile, wink, bright surprise, playful concentration, and unguarded delight.**

Unlike Kotono, Mei does not usually conceal emotion. Unlike Nagisa, her embarrassment is not the default romantic mode. Mei’s face is usually in the middle of an action: laughing, calling, eating, performing, inviting, noticing, or being startled by something she just caused.

The art also makes her unusually tactile. She is surrounded by things to hold, eat, chase, pull, throw, present, or play with. Donuts, cats, fireworks, makeup tools, water guns, shopping carts, presents, bouquets, microphones, and plush-like objects all turn her visual world into a field of interaction.

This supports her characterization perfectly. Mei’s feelings become behavior.

---

# Public idol image

Mei’s public idol image is **approachable, energetic, participatory, and contagiously cheerful**.

She does not visually read as the unreachable stage goddess. Her appeal is closer to: “come play,” “come cheer,” “come eat,” “come laugh,” “come make this moment bigger with me.” She is the kind of idol whose charm depends on shrinking the distance between performer and audience.

That matches the transcript material where fans describe her as friend-like and easy to approach. The visual design strongly supports that. Mei’s cards repeatedly place the viewer in the path of her energy: she offers donuts, reaches out a hand, splashes water, smiles directly, or appears close enough to be part of the same event.

At the same time, Mei is not limited to cute genki idol work. The stage cards prove she can handle high-impact performance concepts: Moon Tempest lunar stagewear, cool dance-unit styling, punk/neon underground imagery, and dramatic festival compositions. Her idol image is flexible because her core is not a fixed genre; it is **activation**.

Her public identity is:

**the idol who makes the audience want to move with her.**

---

# Private / casual design cues

Mei’s private/casual cards are some of the clearest sources for her everyday self.

The school/sports-cat image is especially representative: casual athletic clothing, school items, cats climbing on her, a slightly surprised expression, and a bright outdoor environment. It captures Mei as a girl whose daily life naturally becomes incident: she goes somewhere, cats appear, objects scatter, and suddenly the scene becomes a story.

The makeup/casual-room card shows a more social, girl-life version of Mei. She is leaning over a table with cosmetics, smiling, surrounded by small objects, snacks, and other girls. This supports her as someone who learns through group participation: try makeup, ask questions, play, get distracted, make the ordinary event lively.

The shopping-cart/pair card and donut-related images frame her as consumer-space energy: shops, snacks, casual movement, and playful abundance. Unlike Kotono, whose private images often reveal hidden softness beneath dignity, Mei’s private images reveal that her public cheer is not fake. She really is like this. The idol stage amplifies her; it does not invent her.

Her casual design cues are:

**sportswear, food props, cats, loose energetic hair, open smiles, ordinary group settings, and physical messiness.**

She is visually credible as the girl who forgets her lunch, chases cats, gets scolded by Saki, drags Suzu into schemes, and then somehow makes the whole day more fun.

---

# Design archetypes evoked

## The genki idol

Mei is strongly within the 元気っ子 / genki-kko archetype: high energy, loud voice, immediate friendliness, physical enthusiasm, and emotional transparency. But the card art makes her more than a stock genki girl by giving her athletic motion, food motifs, and repeated serious-performance variants.

## The friend-like idol

Her visuals are unusually close and inviting. She does not only smile from a stage; she reaches toward the viewer. This supports her “friend-like and approachable” idol identity.

## The food-motif mascot idol

The donut imagery is central. Mei is not just associated with donuts as a joke; donuts become a whole visual language of circles, sharing, sweetness, reward, and audience connection.

## The cat magnet / animal friend

The cat cards and cat props reinforce her boundary-loose, affectionate, semi-magical relationship with animals and strange phenomena. Cats belong around Mei because she treats the world as immediately social.

## The sporty kinetic idol

Her body language often emphasizes legs, jumping, twisting, skating, swimming, dancing, and running. She feels more physically driven than Nagisa and less ceremonially composed than Kotono.

## The summer-sun idol inside a moon unit

Even within Moon Tempest, Mei visually brings summer, water, fireworks, sunlit skin, festival warmth, and bright primary colors. She is the unit’s warmest element.

## The chaos gremlin with sincerity

Some cards push her toward comic chaos: cats, donuts, shopping carts, Haruhi-style weirdness, underground neon absurdity. But the expressions remain sincere rather than smug. Mei causes chaos because she is earnest, not because she is malicious.

## The gap-serious performer

Wedding, Valentine, rock, and underground cards show her gap appeal. Mei can suddenly become intense, romantic-adjacent, cool, or ceremonial — but the image always retains a trace of open-hearted Mei.

---

# How the design supports personality and story role

Mei’s design supports her writing almost perfectly because it visualizes her central psychological pattern:

**emotion → motion → contact.**

When Mei is happy, the composition opens.
When Mei performs, the frame tilts and accelerates.
When Mei celebrates, props multiply.
When Mei cares, she reaches.
When Mei is serious, the usual brightness compresses into direct eye contact.

The recurring diagonal compositions are especially important. Mei rarely occupies the frame like a stable monument. She cuts across it. That is exactly how she functions narratively: she interrupts stiffness, breaks tension, pulls people into action, and converts stuck feeling into movement.

Her food/cat/summer motifs are not decorative. They correspond to her character architecture:

-   **Donuts** represent reward, sharing, appetite, circles, fan contact, and “everyone together.”

-   **Cats** represent instinctive affection, unpredictability, porousness, and her ability to socialize with anything alive or almost-alive.

-   **Water/summer/fireworks** represent bodily joy, seasonal immediacy, and the beauty of fleeting fun.

-   **Neon/rock/underground styling** represents her openness to idol energy outside polished official forms.

-   **Wedding/Valentine imagery** represents her category-unstable but sincere special feelings, especially around future-oriented intimacy.


Most importantly, Mei’s visual design makes her role in Moon Tempest legible. Kotono’s cool lunar dignity can become too heavy; Nagisa’s emotional memory can become too inward; Saki’s discipline can become too rigid; Suzu’s pride can become too theatrical. Mei keeps the group alive in the body. She is the member who makes people laugh, move, eat, play, cheer, and try again.

---

# Relationship to Moon Tempest’s visual language

Mei is visually the least “purely lunar” of the Moon Tempest members, and that is the point.

When she wears Moon Tempest’s darker blue, white, black, or gold stage language, she warms it. Her hair, eye color, expression, and posture stop the palette from becoming solemn. In lunar stage cards, she looks less like the moon itself and more like reflected festival light dancing across water.

This fits her story role: Mei does not contradict Moon Tempest’s moonlight philosophy. She makes it livable. She is the warmth inside the night.

A concise comparison:

-   **Kotono**: moonlight as dignity after darkness.

-   **Nagisa**: moonlight as remembered closeness.

-   **Mei**: moonlight as a night festival — fireworks, laughter, food stalls, waves, and people holding onto a fleeting moment together.


---

# Final visual thesis

**Mei’s design is a language of kinetic sincerity. Her caramel-brown hair, amber-pink eyes, athletic silhouette, open smiles, diagonal poses, and bright candy/summer palettes make her feel like joy physically entering the frame. She is surrounded by donuts, cats, water, fireworks, gifts, and neon because her character is about turning feeling into immediate shared experience.**

**She is not visually built as a distant star. She is built as contact: the idol who reaches out, pulls the viewer into motion, and makes happiness feel participatory. Within Moon Tempest, Mei is the internal sun — not because she replaces the unit’s moonlight, but because she keeps that moonlit world warm, loud, alive, and full of people who still want to smile.**

Yes — **that is very much a recurring comic gag around Mei**, though I would phrase it carefully:

**The gag is not that Mei and Makino are actually canoodling. The gag is that Mei uses “date” language so innocently and casually that other people temporarily read it as suspicious, while Makino/the manager is left insisting it is a misunderstanding.**

The clearest example is in **Decision Of The Underdogs**. Mei sees Makino/{user} with Satomi and immediately yells that they are “dating.” When Satomi panics, Mei then asks Makino to “date” her next time too. Satomi is shocked that Makino is not more flustered by Mei asking him out, and Mei says, basically, “But we go on dates all the time, right?” Makino clarifies that they are just going to convenience stores or supermarkets together. big4\_004\_decision\_of\_the\_underd…

Then the gag escalates: Nagisa reacts to Mei saying “one more time,” reading the situation through a suggestive romance lens, while Saki says that the earlier “Hoshimi City incident” ended as a false accusation, but now that everyone has let their guard down, Makino has “done it again.” She even almost calls him ゲス before correcting herself. Makino protests that this is also a false accusation. big4\_004\_decision\_of\_the\_underd…

So yes, the story explicitly frames this as a **running false-accusation gag**. Saki’s line about the earlier 星見市 incident strongly implies that this is not the first time the group has misread Makino/Mei proximity as something improper. The joke is that Mei’s innocent physical/social closeness creates bad optics, and Saki/Nagisa’s interpretive frameworks make it worse.

But the important nuance is that the “canoodling” suspicion is usually **comedic and externally projected**, not Mei knowingly trying to create a scandal. Mei’s own vocabulary is category-unstable: “date” can mean walking together, shopping together, eating together, being alone together, or doing something fun with someone she likes. Later she outright calls a seaside walk with Makino a “プチデート,” then teases him about whether he thought they looked like a couple. card\_mei\_011\_st-card-mei-05-miz…

So the answer is:

**Yes, one of Mei’s earlier comic gags is that her closeness with Makino gets interpreted as suspiciously date-like. But the joke is not “Mei is sexually forward”; it is “Mei collapses ordinary closeness, fun outings, and romantic date language into the same emotional bucket, and everyone around her reacts as if Makino is in trouble.”**

# Overall contribution to Suzu

These six cards establish Suzu along several major axes.

First, Suzu’s pride is not fake, but it is **fragile pride**. She genuinely believes she is special, but she also panics when evidence suggests others may see her as childish, amateurish, or unimportant. The Kyoto card is built around this: being called “お嬢ちゃん” by a shopkeeper wounds her because she wants adult elegance and 色気 / iro-ke, “mature allure,” not child treatment. card\_suz\_005\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

Second, Suzu is deeply shaped by **role anxiety**. The Alice card is the clearest example: she lies by omission because she wants everyone to believe she once played Alice, when in truth she played a tree and was mocked by classmates. The story reveals that her grand performance persona covers a very specific wound: she learned as a child that a “small role” could become a source of humiliation. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

Third, Suzu’s family background matters enormously. Her father is indulgent and adoring; her mother is strict and skeptical of idolhood. That split explains much of Suzu’s personality: she has been pampered enough to expect special treatment, but pressured enough to need achievement as proof. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

Fourth, Suzu’s Mana worship is not passive fandom. She studies Mana’s commercials, lines, MCs, and public presence almost archivally. But unlike Kotono, Mana is not a grief-wound; unlike Mei, Mana is not mainly remembered warmth. For Suzu, Mana is **the gold standard of idol radiance**, the sacred image she wants to approximate and eventually succeed. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

Fifth, the batch clarifies Suzu’s comic machinery: she misunderstands, overdramatizes, imitates first and understands later, panics when romance or ghosts appear, becomes intoxicated by praise, and converts food obsessions into grand idol projects. But crucially, she almost always works hard once the fantasy attaches to a goal.

# 1\. 「あんなのぴょーんですわ」 — the tree role, shame, and the dignity of every part

**Importance tier: Core.**

This is the most important card in the batch for Suzu’s deeper psychology.

The premise is simple: Suzu has a double-leading role as Alice alongside Shizuku in an Alice-themed stage. She immediately inflates this into a grand claim: now that she is BIG4, she is “大女優成宮すず,” the great actress Narumiya Suzu. She claims she already played Alice as a child and that the stage was a triumph because everyone was captivated by her. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

The comedy comes from how obvious it becomes that something is wrong. She sweats when asked for video evidence. She reacts too knowledgeably when told Shizuku once played a tree. She defends the tree role with suspiciously personal detail: standing still is hard, the leaves itch, and it is practically craftsmanship. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

The reveal is emotionally important: Suzu never played Alice. She played a tree. Her parents loudly cheered for her, which was encouraging to her, but her classmates mocked her afterward. As a child, she concluded that playing a tree — a minor role — was shameful. So when she finally gets the Alice role, she wants it as a kind of revenge against the old humiliation. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

This reframes Suzu’s “I must be the star” energy. It is not only vanity. It is a reaction against an old lesson: **small roles get laughed at**.

The manager’s correction is crucial. He tells her that every role in a stage matters, that no one is unnecessary, and that the old video showed her standing proudly and seriously. He also connects the tree role and her earlier “少女A” role to her current success: because Suzu takes every role seriously, she succeeded as Alice. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

That gives Suzu one of her core developmental lessons:

**Being important does not always mean being the center. A role can be small and still be real.**

Suzu’s comic ending — deciding that next time she should play a giant tree as the protagonist — is perfect because she has not become modest in a conventional way. She has absorbed the lesson through Suzu logic: if the tree was important, then make the tree magnificent.

**What it adds:**
This card establishes Suzu’s deepest insecurity: being reduced to a side role, laughed at, or treated as non-central. It also gives her a healthier path: pride not as denial of small roles, but as the ability to make any role grand through effort.

---

# 2\. 「念願のCM」 — Mana worship, romantic panic, and potato-chip professionalism

**Importance tier: Important to core.**

This card is both very funny and very revealing.

Suzu begins by rewatching Mana’s Valentine CM, reciting lines from memory, and becoming too embarrassed to finish the most romantic part. She also says that as a Mana fan she remembers not only commercials, but memorable MC and TV talk moments. This establishes Suzu as an idol scholar of Mana’s image, at least in her own obsessive way. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

The offer of a snack commercial immediately becomes, in Suzu’s head, “my turn to stand in the Mana/TRINITYAiLE lineage.” The actual product is potato chips, but because it airs around Valentine’s, she fixates on Valentine chocolate and romantic commercial grammar. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

The middle section is a classic Suzu spiral. Influenced by Nagisa’s romance drama, she imagines that a kiss-adjacent performance could destabilize her relationship with the manager. She argues that if she shows him a kiss expression, he may fall desperately in love with her. She then misreads a potato-chip “duck mouth” gag as some sort of depraved childhood eroticism, leading to rumors about the manager around Hoshimi. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

This matters because it shows Suzu’s romantic consciousness in a different register from Nagisa and Mei.

Nagisa understands romance as narrative and genre.
Mei experiences closeness before naming it.
Suzu treats romance as a dangerously theatrical force that could suddenly overthrow social order.

She is naïve, but not uninterested. She is highly suggestible to melodrama: once she watches a drama about friendship turning romantic through a kiss scene, she applies that logic to herself and the manager.

The final section is important because the misunderstanding does not erase her professionalism. Once she realizes it is a potato-chip CM, she is embarrassed, apologizes, and then fully commits because potato chips are something she genuinely loves. Her ad-libs are vivid and successful, and she later gives the manager chocolate-coated potato chips for Valentine’s. She still wants a future chocolate CM like Mana and leaves the manager with a lightly provocative question about how he would react if she performed a kiss scene. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

**What it adds:**
Suzu’s Mana fandom, romantic innocence, and food comedy all converge into idol work. She can be absurdly wrong about the premise and still produce a good performance once she locks onto the job.

---

# 3\. 「アメリカ留学」 — family pressure, solo-work anxiety, and professional grit

**Importance tier: Core.**

This is the other core card in the batch, alongside Alice.

The opening ties Suzu back to the Rei-family event: she brags that she solved Rei’s problem by marching into Rei’s home and lecturing her father about idol work. Then the conversation turns to Suzu’s own family. Her father is indulgent, doting, and now apparently proud enough to show off her CDs, goods, and photos to visitors. Her mother, by contrast, remains strict and has previously tried to make Suzu quit idol work and study abroad. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

This family split is essential. Suzu is not simply spoiled. She is both spoiled and judged. Her father’s adoration inflates her; her mother’s skepticism sharpens her insecurity.

When her mother emails that Suzu is less visible than the other girls and should go to America to start over, Suzu first reacts with outrage — but then realizes there is truth in the criticism. Others are gaining solo work through centers, hobbies, radio, gravure, or personal appeal, while Suzu has been coasting somewhat. She decides to take on any offer, even work she would normally refuse. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

The second section is a crucial maturation step. Suzu admits that earlier she was partly resisting study abroad out of stubbornness, but now she wants to continue idol work because she loves it: she has performed with the others, stood on big stages, and been supported by fans. She also says she cannot let herself be left behind while others advance. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

This makes the card a pivot from **rebellion** to **positive vocation**. Suzu no longer wants to remain an idol merely to defy her mother. She wants to remain an idol because idolhood has become her chosen life.

The small-job montage is especially important. She distributes flyers for a shopping-mall event, hosts a character show, helps with merch, and explicitly says that steadily raising name recognition is important idol work. She defines a true idol as someone who gives full effort to even small jobs. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

The final gravure shoot then tests that philosophy. Exhausted and paranoid, Suzu convinces herself the shoot is a prank. But the misunderstanding accidentally enables courage: believing she must “perform” against a hidden prank, she talks back to a famous intimidating photographer, insists that her charm has not yet been captured, and pushes the shoot to higher intensity. The photographer praises her nerve; her mother, who was secretly watching, acknowledges that Suzu has grown through idol work. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

This is a perfect Suzu mechanism:

**misunderstanding → overconfidence → real courage → real result.**

**What it adds:**
This card establishes Suzu’s professional growth arc: from pampered, threatened girl to idol who can do small jobs, face adults, and prove through work that idolhood has made her stronger.

---

# 4\. 「そわそわの理由」 — birthday, princess fantasy, and being loved by the group

**Importance tier: Important.**

This card is comedic, but it gives valuable social texture.

Suzu receives a birthday-feature project and immediately turns it into a grand “Narumiya Suzu birth festival,” complete with solo stage, fan quiz, commemorative photo, possibly elephants, and eventually cosmic-scale ambitions. Her imagination is excessive, but it is also fan-directed: she wants a special event where fans can gather around her as the day’s protagonist. card\_suz\_004\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

The more important part is Mei and Shizuku’s response. They recognize that Suzu is excited and working hard, so they create the “Suzu-nyan Princess Plan,” treating her like a princess before the event with tea, sweets, idol DVDs, potato chips, aroma, and massage. The plan is silly, but their underlying motive is sincere: Suzu is preparing late into the night for fans, and they want to make her happy in return. card\_suz\_004\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

Suzu, of course, initially interprets their behavior as them finally recognizing her overwhelming aura. This is very Suzu: affection is immediately translated into status confirmation. But after the event, when Mei and Shizuku confess they were trying to make her birthday fun, she accepts that their feelings were real even if the “princess” treatment was exaggerated. She admits she got carried away because she was so happy. card\_suz\_004\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

The ending is surprisingly tender. Mei and Shizuku tell Suzu that helping with the birthday made them like her even more: the way she planned carefully for fans, the way she became delighted, the way she is unexpectedly shy when praised, and the way she loves everyone at Hoshimi. Suzu becomes overwhelmed and embarrassed. card\_suz\_004\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

That final embarrassment matters. Suzu can handle being worshipped abstractly as “Suzu-sama,” but direct, specific affection from friends destabilizes her. She wants praise, but real love makes her flustered.

**What it adds:**
Suzu wants to be treated as special, but what actually moves her is being seen by friends who understand how hard she works and how much she loves the group.

---

# 5\. 「慰安旅行 in京都」 — childishness, adult allure, and learning through imitation

**Importance tier: Important texture.**

This Kyoto birthday-trip card is not as psychologically central as Alice or America, but it is very useful for Suzu’s self-image.

The initial Suzu is familiar: extravagant travel preferences, food obsession, fear of ghosts, impatience, and dependence on the manager’s planning. But the Gion section gives the card its real meaning. A shopkeeper treats her like a little girl shopping with her older brother and gives her an extra dango. Suzu is deeply offended because she is fifteen and insists she is not that childish. card\_suz\_005\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

This is a small gag, but it connects to a major Suzu concern: **being seen as a child**.

She then sees a maiko and becomes captivated by adult grace, elegance, and 色気. When told maiko may be around her age and are training through strict communal discipline, she compares them to Hoshimi idols. That comparison is important because it shows Suzu trying to process idol maturity through another performance tradition: young girls training into professional elegance. card\_suz\_005\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

Her first move is, of course, imitation. She adopts awkward Kyoto speech, calls the manager `{user}はん`, uses “どす,” and declares that “Narumiya Suzu plus Kyoto dialect” is unstoppable. The manager gently corrects her: language alone is not the point; she could learn dance softness, graceful movement, and consideration instead. card\_suz\_005\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

This is a very useful Suzu pattern:

1.  She sees an ideal.

2.  She imitates its most visible surface.

3.  She overclaims mastery.

4.  Someone reframes the deeper transferable skill.

5.  She partially accepts the correction while preserving her pride.


**What it adds:**
Suzu’s adult-image anxiety is explicit. She wants maturity, 色気, and elegance, but tends to reach for costume and speech first. Her growth depends on moving from surface imitation to embodied understanding.

---

# 6\. 「めちゃくちゃですわー」 — potato chips, private chaos, and birthday play

**Importance tier: Useful flavor / important comic texture.**

This card is mostly comedic, but it sharpens Suzu’s everyday texture.

The morning sequence shows Suzu dreaming about inventing a potato-chip tree, then sleep-attacking Kotono as a potato monster. The room is messy, there are chips on the floor, and Kotono’s surprise operation collapses into slapstick. card\_suz\_006\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

The daytime sequence with Aoi is excellent because it shows another person manipulating Suzu’s fantasy logic. Aoi stages a “potato chip tree” surprise, and Suzu half-doubts but half-believes because the dream has primed her. She is thrilled by the impossible abundance, especially when limited flavors appear. card\_suz\_006\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

The nighttime sequence with Haruko is the best part for characterization. Suzu is still awake because the birthday party was too fun. Haruko creates a secret afterparty with tea, cake, potato chips, and a “two-person secret,” and Suzu becomes excited by the small transgression. Her potato-chip taste discrimination is so strong that Haruko calls her a “potato doctor,” and the two improvise a ridiculous potato song until Saki’s silent judgment scares them. card\_suz\_006\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

This card adds little deep wound material, but it strongly confirms Suzu’s private self:

-   messy room;

-   food dreams;

-   fantasy susceptibility;

-   delight in being celebrated;

-   fear of Saki’s judgment;

-   and the ability to turn snacks into performance concepts.


**What it adds:**
Suzu’s food gag is not just appetite. Potato chips are part of her self-mythology: commercial dreams, CM ambition, birthday surprises, secret afterparties, songs, and “I can identify every flavor” pride.

---

# Cross-card synthesis: Suzu after this batch

## 1\. Suzu’s grandiosity is armor, aspiration, and performance style

Suzu says she is a miracle, princess, actress, CM queen, future world-class idol, and someone no one should underestimate. That is funny, but it is not meaningless. Her self-mythologizing performs three functions.

It protects her from shame.
It pushes her into action.
It gives her a public idol language.

When Suzu is scared, embarrassed, or insecure, she often gets louder and more theatrical. That does not make the confidence false; it means confidence is something she performs until she can inhabit it.

## 2\. Her core wound is being made “small”

Several cards circle the same fear:

-   being treated as a child in Kyoto;

-   being remembered as a tree rather than Alice;

-   being told by her mother that she is not visible enough;

-   being less advanced than other members in solo work;

-   being laughed at for a role she took seriously.


Suzu wants to be big because she has felt small.

That is the key to her comedy. Every “I am the greatest” declaration is partly a refusal to return to the place where others laughed at her or decided she could not make it.

## 3\. Her relationship to work is maturing

Early Suzu wants big, flashy, status-confirming jobs. But the America card shows real growth: she learns that small mall events, flyers, character shows, merch support, and local fan interaction are all idol work. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

This is one of the most important shifts for her:

**Suzu begins wanting work that proves she is special, then starts learning that doing ordinary work seriously is what makes her special.**

## 4\. Her family dynamic explains the split inside her

Her father validates her extravagantly; her mother doubts and pressures her. This creates a split between:

-   “I am the world’s cutest idol.”

-   “Maybe I am not proving enough.”


That split powers much of Suzu’s behavior. She wants to be praised like her father praises her, but she also wants to defeat her mother’s skepticism through undeniable achievement.

## 5\. Mana is Suzu’s sacred template

Suzu studies Mana as if Mana were an idol scripture. She memorizes lines, replays CM scenes, and measures her own opportunities against Mana’s precedent. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

This differs from Kotono and Mei. Kotono must separate Mana-as-sister from Mana-as-idol. Mei remembers Mana as warmth and shared experience. Suzu begins with Mana as ideal image: polished, romantic, adored, professional, unforgettable.

Her growth will likely involve learning not only to imitate Mana’s surface, but to identify what made Mana’s idolhood meaningful underneath.

## 6\. Suzu’s femininity is theatrical and anxious

Kyoto and Valentine both show Suzu wanting adult femininity but being unsure how to handle it.

With maiko, she imitates speech because she wants elegance and 色気.
With Valentine, she imagines romantic/kiss performance as socially explosive and personally dangerous.
With the shopkeeper, she is wounded by child treatment.
With the manager, she sometimes teases or imagines future romantic impact, but then panics or overdramatizes.

Suzu is not indifferent to romance or maturity. She is fascinated by them, but her understanding is still theatrical, borrowed, and easily overheated.

## 7\. Her friends know how to handle her

Mei, Shizuku, Haruko, Aoi, Kotono, Saki, and the manager all reveal different Suzu-handling modes:

-   Mei and Shizuku inflate her ego because they know it makes her happy, but later ground it in real affection.

-   Aoi plays with her fantasy logic through the potato-chip tree.

-   Haruko indulges her secret afterparty energy.

-   Kotono tries to surprise her but gets dragged into slapstick.

-   Saki’s silent judgment terrifies her.

-   The manager reframes her surface imitation into deeper skill.


Suzu is ridiculous, but she is also loved. The group’s ability to tease, indulge, correct, and celebrate her is part of why her grandiosity does not become alienating.

---

# Importance tiers

## Core

**あんなのぴょーんですわ**
Essential Suzu psychology. Defines her shame around small roles, her desire for star status, her parents’ overenthusiastic support, and the lesson that every role has dignity. card\_suz\_001\_st-card-suz-05-ali…

**アメリカ留学**
Core for family pressure, mother conflict, solo-work anxiety, overwork, professional maturation, small-job seriousness, and the way Suzu can turn mistaken assumptions into real courage. card\_suz\_003\_st-card-suz-05-anm…

**念願のCM**
Important for Mana worship, CM ambition, romantic immaturity, manager-related overinterpretation, and the conversion of potato-chip fandom into real idol work. card\_suz\_002\_st-card-suz-05-ang…

**そわそわの理由**
Important for Suzu’s need to be celebrated, Mei/Shizuku’s affection, birthday-event professionalism, and the distinction between shallow worship and real friendship. card\_suz\_004\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

**慰安旅行 in京都**
Important for adult-image anxiety, maiko imitation, food/ghost comedy, and the pattern of surface imitation becoming deeper learning. card\_suz\_005\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

**めちゃくちゃですわー**
Useful comic texture for private Suzu: potato-chip fantasy, messiness, birthday excitement, Aoi/Haruko dynamics, and fear of Saki’s judgment. card\_suz\_006\_st-card-suz-05-bir…

# Updated Suzu thesis after this batch

**Narumiya Suzu is Moon Tempest’s theatrical pride: an ojou-sama idol whose enormous self-confidence is both real and compensatory. She wants to be the miracle, the princess, the actress, the CM queen, the unforgettable center of attention — but that desire is rooted in a fear of being treated as childish, laughed at, left behind, or assigned a “small role” that no one values.**

**Her growth is not learning to stop being dramatic. It is learning to make the drama true: to take every role seriously, to do small jobs with full effort, to transform embarrassment into pride, to accept affection without needing to convert it into worship, and to become an idol whose magnificence is earned rather than merely declared.**

This Suzu batch is **very important**, and it pushes her beyond the first-pass model of “theatrical pride covering insecurity.” That remains true, but these cards add a warmer, more relational, and more mature version of Suzu:

> **Suzu is not only trying to prove that she is special. # Overall contribution to Suzu

This batch adds six major dimensions.

First, **Suzu’s early idol insecurity is now explicit**. In 「背伸びしてる？」, she worries about her body compared with Mana and rejects the idea that Mana could have weaknesses. In 「成宮すず、ここにあり！」, she spirals because early online comments mention Kotono and Nagisa but not her. Her “I am the center” outburst is funny, but it is also the earliest form of her fear: *what if I am invisible?* card\_suz\_009\_st-card-suz-05-ido…

Second, **her desire for ordinary youth becomes clearer**. The K-ON collaboration card is crucial: after performing with 放課後ティータイム, Suzu becomes quietly jealous of their club life. She says she prioritizes becoming a top idol, but admits she wonders, just a little, whether she might have had that kind of youth too. card\_suz\_010\_st-card-suz-05-kio…

Third, **Suzu’s friendship anxiety is surprisingly strong**. She may speak like an imperious ojou-sama, but when she wants to befriend 放課後ティータイム, she becomes hesitant, worried, and deeply afraid of being disliked. Her actual message, when stripped of pride, is extremely direct: she loves them, had fun, wants to see them again, and wants to become closer. card\_suz\_010\_st-card-suz-05-kio…

Fourth, **Mei/Suzu gains more weight**. The “わんにゃんツインズ” card confirms that Suzu and Mei are not just a gag duo. Suzu gets jealous of Mei’s other friends, becomes embarrassed when Mei calls her beloved, converts terror into a stage concept, and ends by telling Mei that whatever happens, she considers her a friend and wants to go back together. card\_suz\_011\_st-card-suz-05-lin…

Fifth, **the beach card gives Suzu a real childhood deprivation note**. Her overexcitement about the sea is not merely spoiled childishness. She says that, at home, she had lessons every day and was not allowed to go play at the sea with friends. The swimsuit/MV card turns a gag about leisure goods into a serious statement: Suzu has been performing adulthood and excellence while still longing for missed ordinary play. card\_suz\_012\_st-card-suz-05-miz…

Sixth, **「思い入れのある曲」 is probably the most important Suzu card in this batch**. It shows her becoming a real pillar of Moon Tempest. Her desire for center is not only vanity. For the four-member song created during Kotono’s absence, Suzu sees herself as the one who can carry its feeling most deeply. She wants to illuminate Kotono as Kotono once illuminated them. That is a major maturation of her rival/support role. card\_suz\_007\_st-card-suz-05-fes…
